                                                      Spectrum of Random d-regular Graphs Up to the Edge
                                                                            Jiaoyang Huang∗1 and Horng-Tzer Yau†2
                                                                                    1
                                                                                        University of Pennsylvania
                                                                                          2
                                                                                            Harvard University


                                                                                                 Abstract
                                                     Consider the normalized adjacency matrices of random d-regular graphs on N vertices with fixed
                                                 degree d ⩾ 3. We prove that, with probability 1 − N −1+ε for any ε > 0, the following two properties
                                                 hold as N → ∞ provided that d ⩾ 3: (i) The eigenvalues are close to the classical eigenvalue locations




arXiv:2102.00963v3 [math.PR] 2 Feb 2025
                                                 given by the Kesten-McKay distribution. In particular, the extremal eigenvalues are concentrated with
                                                 polynomial error bound in N , i.e. λ2 , |λN | ⩽ 2 + N −c . (ii) All eigenvectors of random d-regular graphs
                                                 are completely delocalized.

                                          1      Introduction and Main Results
                                          1.1     Introduction
                                          The random d-regular graph ensemble is defined to be the set GN,d of (simple) d-regular graphs on N vertices
                                          equipped with uniform probability. It is a fundamental model of sparse random graphs and it arises naturally
                                          in many different contexts. The spectral properties of the adjacency matrices of random d-regular graphs,
                                          i.e. eigenvalues and eigenvectors, are of particular interest in computer science, combinatorics, and statistical
                                          physics. The relevant topics include the theory of expanders (see e.g. [72]), quantum chaos (see e.g. [73]),
                                          and graph ζ-functions (see e.g. [78]).
                                               Throughout the paper, we fix d ⩾ 3, and denote A = A(G) the adjacency matrix of a random d-regular
                                          graph G on P N vertices. Thus A is uniformly chosen among all symmetric N × N matrices with    √ entries in
                                          {0, 1} with j Aij = d and Aii = 0 for all i. We normalize the adjacency matrix as H = A/ d − 1, and
                                                                            √
                                          denote its eigenvalues by λ1 = d/ d − 1 ⩾ λ2 ⩾ · · · ⩾ λN . The constant vector is a trivial eigenvector to
                                              ∗ huangjy@wharton.upenn.edu
                                              † htyau@g.harvard.edu




                                                                                                     1
A with eigenvalue d. The adjacency matrices for random d-regular graphs are thus random matrices, albeit
the matrix entries are not independent.
     The spectral properties of random matrices with independent entries are well-understood. For example,
the global and local spectral statistics of the generalized Wigner matrices were analyzed in [14, 16, 27, 28,
31, 32, 35, 36, 38, 54, 57, 58, 75, 76]. These results were extended to sparse matrices and Erdős-Rényi random
graphs [28, 30, 45–47, 50–52, 59]. In addition to eigenvalue statistics, delocalization of eigenvectors are often
established provided the Green’s function method was used.
    Since matrix entries of random d-regular graphs are dependent, much less was known regarding their
spectral statistics. If the degree d is fixed, macroscopic eigenvalue statistics were studied using the techniques
of Poisson approximation of short cycles [24,55] and a (nonrigorous) replica method [67]. These results showed
that the macroscopic eigenvalue statistics for random d-regular graphs of fixed degree are different from those
of a Gaussian ensemble. The local eigenvalue statistics, however, were conjectured to be the same as the
Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble statistics [53, 68, 72]. It was known that the spectral density of H converges
to the Kesten–McKay distribution with density given by
                                                               −1 p
                                                            x2        [4 − x2 ]+
                                              
                                                     1
                                    ρd (x) = 1 +         −                       ,                            (1.1)
                                                   d−1       d           2π

on spectral scales (log N )−c [6,25,44,80]. When the degree d grows faster than sufficiently high power of log N ,
the eigenvalue rigidity down to the scale (log N )4 /N (notice that, compared with previous works, the scale
was greatly improved to essentially the optimal one) was established in [11]. Using this result as an input,
bulk universality and the normality of bulk eigenvectors in the same regime was proven in [8, 15], and edge
universality for degrees d ∈ [N 1/3+ε , N 2/3−ε ] was proven in [9]. In a joint paper with R. Bauerschmidt [10],
we extended the convergence of spectral density to scale (log N )c /N when d ⩾ d0 for some large d0 fixed.
Spectral properties of directed d-regular graphs have also been studied recently [22, 23, 49, 61, 66].
    The second largest eigenvalue λ2 of d-regular graphs is of particular interest in theoretical computer
science and combinatorics [20, 48]. The spectral gap, the gap between the first and second eigenvalues,
measures the expanding property of the graph. In [63], Lubotzky, Phillips, and Sarnak defined Ramanujan
graphs, as d-regular graphs with all non-trivial eigenvalues of H bounded by 2. Since limN →∞ λ2 ⩾ 2 for any
deterministic family of d regular graphs on N vertices [1], Ramanujan graphs asymptotically have the largest
possible spectral gap among d-regular graphs. Ramanujan graphs have been a focus of substantial works
in theoretical computer science [19, 56, 71] and mathematics [69, 77]. Proving the existence of Ramanujan
graphs with a large number of vertices is however a difficult task, which was only recently solved for arbitrary
d ⩾ 3 [64, 65]. On the other end, it was conjectured by Alon [1] and proven by Friedman [41] and Bordenave
[12] that with high probability random d-regular graphs are weakly Ramanujan, i.e. λ2 , |λN | ⩽ 2 + o(1).
Their proofs are based on sophisticated moment methods, and the error term in eigenvalue is of order
O((log log N/ log N )2 ).
    It was conjectured that the distribution of the second largest eigenvalue after normalizing by N 2/3 is the
same as that of the largest eigenvalue of the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble [68, 72]; i.e., there is a constant
cN,d so that N 2/3 (λ2 −2)−cN,d has the Tracy-Widom distribution [79]. This would imply that the fluctuations
of extreme eigenvalues are of order O(N −2/3 ) if cN,d is of order one. If cN,d = 0 (which seems to be the
most probable scenario), then it would imply that more than half of all d-regular graphs are Ramanujan
graphs, namely d-regular graphs with λ2 ⩽ 2. As a first step towards this conjecture, in this article we
prove a polynomial error bound for extremal eigenvalues: with high probability λ2 , |λN | ⩽ 2 + O(N −c )
for some c > 0. This gives a new proof of Alon’s conjecture [1] with an O(N −c ) error bound instead of
O((log log N/ log N )2 ) errors in [12, 41]. In addition to this estimate on the second largest eigenvalue, our
results imply estimates on all eigenvalues in the spectrum, see Theorem 1.2.
     As discrete analogues of compact negatively curved surfaces [18], d-regular graphs provide a good ground
to explore the phenomenon of delocalization of eigenvectors. For d-regular graphs with locally tree-like
structure, it was known that the eigenvectors v of H are weakly delocalized in the sense that their entries
are uniformly bounded by (log N )−c ∥v∥2 [18,25,44] and their ℓ2 -mass cannot concentrate on a small set [18].
If, in addition, the graphs are expanders, the eigenvectors of H also satisfy the quantum ergodicity property
[5,6,17]. In [10], we proved that the bulk eigenvectors of random d-regular graphs are completely delocalized
with high probability for d ⩾ d0 for some large d0 fixed. In this paper, we extend this result to any d ⩾ 3
and to all eigenvectors including edge eigenvectors. Our results imply in particular that the eigenvectors


                                                        2
of random 3-regular graphs are completely delocalized with probability 1 − N −1+ε . We will explain later
on in this section that the error probability 1 − N −1+ε is optimal in the sense that there are events with
probability N −1+ε so that some eigenvectors are localized. Our proof will also show that the existence of
too many cycles in a small neighborhood is the main reason for the localization of eigenvectors.
    All our results on both eigenvalues and eigenvectors will be derived from a high probability estimate on
the Green’s function G(z) = (H −z)−1 of the normalized adjacency matrix for random d-regular graphs. The
trace of the Green’s function gives the Stieltjes transform of empirical eigenvalue distribution, which encodes
the information of eigenvalue locations. Delocalization of all eigenvectors follows from estimates on the
entries of the Green’s function provided that the imaginary part of the spectral parameter can be chosen to
be nearly optimal, i.e., Im z ≳ N −1+ε . The recent self-consistent Green’s function method [33, 34, 37, 39, 59],
invented for Wigner matrices, was primarily applied to matrices with (nearly) independent entires. It is
a powerful tool to obtain spectral information for the full range of spectrum. Our key observation is to
construct a novel functional of Green’s function satisfying a simple self-consistent equation. This allows
us to derive estimates on Green’s function so that all results in this paper for random regular graphs with
degree up to the optimal d ⩾ 3 are simple corollary of these estimates. The correlations of entries in H will
cause major difficulties; these will be explained later in this section.


1.2    Statements of main results: eigenvalue statistics
In this paper we fix a parameter 0 < c < 1 (e.g. c = 0.99). Let R = (c/4) logd−1 N . For each d ⩾ 3, we
associate an integer ωd > 0 (see Definition 2.6 for the precise definition) so that, roughly speaking, an infinite
d-regular graph with excess (the smallest number of edges that must be removed to yield a graph with no
cycles) less than or equal to ωd has good spectral properties.

Definition 1.1. We define the set of radius-R tree like graphs, Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d , to consist of graphs such that
   • the radius-R neighborhood of any vertex has excess at most ωd ;
   • the number of vertices whose radius-R neighborhood that contains a cycle is at most N c .
    Our main results, to be stated in the rest of this section, assert that on the set Ω̄ with probability
1 − N −C for any C > 0, the eigenvalues of any d-regular graphs are near their classical locations and the
corresponding eigenvectors are completely delocalized.

Theorem 1.2 (Eigenvalue Rigidity). Fix d ⩾ 3, 0 < c < 1, R = (c/4) logd−1 N and recall the set of radius-R
tree like graphs Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition 1.1. For any large C > 0 and N large enough, with probability
1 − O(N −C ) with respect to the uniform measure on Ω̄, the eigenvalues of G ∈ Ω̄ satisfy:

                                       |λi − γi | ⩽ N −Ω(1) ,    2 ⩽ i ⩽ N,                                  (1.2)

where γi are classical eigenvalue locations given by the Kesten-McKay distribution (1.1)
                                        Z 2
                                                           i
                                              ρd (x)dx =     ,   2 ⩽ i ⩽ N.
                                         γi                N


     Thanks to Proposition 2.1, most d-regular graphs are locally tree-like according to Definition 1.1 in
the sense that P(Ω̄) ⩾ 1 − N −(1−c)ωd . Theorem 1.2 thus implies that with high probability, the non-
trivial extremal eigenvalues of random d-regular graphs concentrate around ±2. More precisely, we have the
following estimates on the extremal eigenvalues. We remark that the exponent Ω(1) in the following theorem
can be chosen to be 0.01 by inspecting our proof.

Theorem 1.3 (Extremal eigenvalues). Fix d ⩾ 3 and 0 < c < 1. There exists a positive integer ωd , defined
in Definition 2.6, such that
                                      λ2 , |λN | ⩽ 2 + N −Ω(1) ,                                    (1.3)

with probability 1 − O(N −(1−c)ωd ) with respect to the uniform measure on GN,d .

contains the subgraph shown above with probability Ω(N −1 ). Our results imply that the eigenvectors of
random 3-regular graphs are completely delocalized with probability 1 − N −1+o(1) , which is optimal.


1.3    Delocalization of Eigenvectors
For d-regular graphs with locally tree-like structure,
                                                     P the eigenvectors v of H cannot concentrate on a small
set, in the sense that any vertex set V ⊂ [[N ]] with i∈V |vi |2 ⩾ ε∥v∥2 must have at least N c(ε) elements [18].
Moreover, for deterministic locally tree-like d-regular expander graphs, it was
                                                                             P proved that the eigenvectors    v
satisfy a quantum ergodicity property: for all a ∈ RN with ∥a∥∞ ⩽ 1 and i ai = 0, averages of | i ai vi2 |2
                                                                                                       P
over macroscopically many eigenvectors v are close to 0 [5, 6, 17]. Our main results regarding eigenvectors of
sparse d-regular graphs are summarized in the following theorem:

Theorem 1.4 (Eigenvector delocalization). Fix d ⩾ 3, 0 < c < 1, R = (c/4) logd−1 N and recall the set
of radius-R tree-like graphs Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition 1.1. For any large C > 0 and N large enough, with
probability 1 − O(N −C ) with respect to the uniform measure on Ω̄, the eigenvectors of G ∈ Ω̄ satisfy

                                                       (log N )Ω(1)
                                              ∥v∥∞ ⩽       √        ∥v∥2 .                                  (1.4)
                                                             N

    Thanks to Proposition 2.1, P(Ω̄) ⩾ 1 − N (−1+c)ωd . Theorem 1.2 thus implies that, with probability
1 − O(N −(1−c)ωd ) with respect to the uniform measure on GN,d , (1.4) holds. In Theorem 1.4, Ω̄ is the set
of d-regular graphs, which do not have many cycles in a small neighborhood. The existence of too many
cycles in a small neighborhood may result in localized eigenvectors. For example, a random 3-regular graph
containing the subgraph in Figure 1, which happens with probability Ω(N −1 ), has a localized eigenvector.
Using ω3 = 1 from Proposition A.2 and taking c = o(1), we conclude from Theorem 1.4 that the eigenvectors
of random 3-regular graphs are completely delocalized with probability 1 − N −1+o(1) , which is optimal.
However, there do exist infinite families of locally tree-like d-regular graphs with localized eigenvectors, as
constructed in [2, 43]. Finally, we remark that for random d-regular graphs of fixed degree, a Gaussian wave
correlation structure for the eigenvectors was predicted in [26] and partially confirmed in [7].

1.4    Comments on key ideas
The Green’s function of a graph encodes its spectral information. Eigenvalue rigidity and delocalization of
eigenvectors follow from estimates of the Green’s function down to optimal scale. The main difficulty to
estimate Green’s function near the spectral edge is due to the square root singularity of the Kesten-McKay
law near the spectral edge, which is a general phenomenon in random matrix theory. To overcome it, instead
of approximating Green’s function by tree extensions as in [10], we approximate it by extensions of graphs
with certain boundary weights. Informally, the entries of the Green’s function can be interpreted as a sum
over weighted paths:

                                              1 H   H2  H3
                          G(z) = (H − z)−1 = − − 2 − 3 − 4 · · · ,            z ∈ C+ .                      (1.5)
                                              z z    z   z
The ij-th entry of G is a sum over weighted paths from vertex i to vertex j. For any vertex o, to compute
Goo , we need to sum over weighted paths from o to itself. To do that we fix a radius-ℓ (which is of order
log log N , see Definition 4.1) neighborhood of o, and denote it by T (which may not be a tree) and its vertex
set T. Then paths from o to itself either stay completely inside T , or leave at some boundary point of T and
come back. For random d-regular graphs, most vertices have large tree neighborhoods (which contain no

tree with boundary weights Q(G, z).

j and come back at j as in Figure 2. The sum of such weighted paths is the sum (over j) of weighted paths
from j to itself staying outside T . Denote by G (T) the graph G with vertices in T removed and G(T) its

Green’s function. With these notations, the weighted paths from j to itself staying outside T is just Gjj .

in random d-regular graphs have large tree neighborhoods, the paths from j to itself not containing i are

    The quantity we will use to derive a self-consistent equation is the average of Gjj for all pairs of adjacent
vertices i ∼ j:
                                                                   1 X (i)

For d-regular graphs, the quantity Q(G, z), although complicated, turns out to be a more fundamental
object than the Stieltjes transform of the empirical measure of the eigenvalues of the matrix H. To compute
  (i)
Gjj , we can approximate it by the Green’s function of a radius ℓ neighborhood of j with vertex i removed
and with a suitable weight at each boundary vertex. Since most vertices in random d-regular graphs have
large tree neighborhoods, most vertices j in the summation of (1.6) have large tree neighborhoods. For
those vertices j, we can future replace the boundary weights in the approximation by the weight Q(G, z)
(Figure 2). Moreover, the radius ℓ neighborhoods of those vertices j with vertex i removed are truncated
(d − 1)-ary trees of depth ℓ. Let Yℓ (Q(G, z), z) be the Green’s function at root vertex of a depth ℓ truncated
(d − 1)-ary tree with boundary weights Q(G, z). We expect to have the following self-consistent equation of
Q(G, z)

                                                       1 X (i)
                                      Q(G, z) =              G ≈ Yℓ (Q(G, z), z).                                      (1.7)
                                                      N d i∼j jj


    The above heuristics based on expressing Green’s function in terms of a sum over paths, i.e., (1.5), are
not rigorous, because (1.5) does not converge for z close to the spectrum. Instead of using this expansion, we
will analyze the self-consistent equations and use heavily the idea of local resampling (see Section 3). The
local resampling in random graphs was known for a long time, but the first implementation to derive the
spectral statistics appeared only recently in [11, Section 3]. The local resampling was modified in [10, Section
7], which randomizes the boundary of a neighborhood T (instead of randomizing edges near a vertex [11])
by switching the edge boundary of T with randomly chosen edges in the remaining of the graph. The local
resampling used in this paper performs a simple switching on a boundary edge of T only when this switching
pair is far away from other switching pairs. While one can put any restrictions on switchings, it is critical in
our application that this restriction preserves the reversibility of the switching, i.e., the law for the graphs
and their switched graphs is exchangeable.


                                                                  5
     More precisely, we will use the exchangeability in the following way. Since the local resampling preserves
uniform distribution on random d-regular graphs, instead of proving (1.7) for G, we prove it for G̃, which
is the graph obtained from G by a local resampling. Since the local resampling is equivalent to taking out
the subgraph T and replanting it back to G at random locations, the new boundary vertices of T after local
resampling are typically far away from each other in the sense of graph distance (Section 5.2) and Green’s
function distance (Section 5.3). Therefore we can rigorously approximate the Green’s function G̃oo of G̃ by
                                                       (i)
the Green’s function of T with boundary weights G̃jj at the boundary vertex j. On the other hand, since
the edge boundary of T contains more than log N edges, the average of the weights at boundary vertices of
T concentrates around Q(G̃, z) (Section 6.1). This allows us to replace boundary weights by the same weight
Q(G̃, z) at each boundary vertex, and leads to the self-consistent equation (1.7).
     While the delocalization of eigenvectors is a direct consequence of (1.7), the error term in the self-
consistent equation (1.7) we will derive is not small enough to detect the locations of extremal eigenvalues,
i.e. λ2 , λN . This is a standard problem in random matrix theory, namely, the self-consistent equation yields
a “weak law” while detecting extremal eigenvalue locations requires some form of a “strong law”. Without
getting into terminology, our goal is to show that the high moments of the self-consistent equation

                                        E[|Q(G, z) − Yℓ (Q(G, z), z)|2p ],                                  (1.8)

are sufficiently small for large integer p > 0. This step is typically called the fluctuation averaging esti-
mate [39,59]. In our setting, the matrix elements of d-regular graphs are not independent and the traditional
method clearly fails. Our key observation is that the independence property can be replaced by the re-
versibility of the local resampling, i.e., the pair (G, G̃) with G̃ denoting the graph obtained from a random
                                                                                    law
d-regular graph G by a local resampling forms an exchangeable
                                                       √          pair, i.e. (G, G̃) = (G̃, G). Besides the lack
of independence, there is an outlier eigenvalue at d/ d − 1 for d-regular graphs. We need to remove the
contribution of the outlier eigenvalue from the self-consistent equation (1.8). With these ideas, we improve
the error bound in the self-consistent equation (1.7) to its square in the high moments sense. With this
improved error bound, we conclude that non-trivial extremal eigenvalues concentrate around ±2.
     Finally, we remark on the difficulties in extending the results [10] for large d all the way to d ⩾ 3 in
this paper. We first note that the spectral property of the graphs for small d are very sensitive to small
perturbations; one can easily break the nice spectral properties, e.g. delocalized eigenvectors and large
spectral gap, by changing a small number of edges. To overcome this, we design the new local resampling to
perform simple switchings only when the switching pair has tree neighborhood and is far away from other
switching pairs. In this way, all the intermediate graphs from local resampling will have good local geometry,
i.e. they don’t have too many cycles in small neighborhoods. Then we can show all the intermediate graphs
have desired spectral properties for any d ⩾ 3. Another major difficulty for small d is that typical estimates
of the Green’s function do not hold with very high probability. For example when d = 3, the delocalization
of eigenvectors hold at most with probability 1 − O(N −1 ) (See Figure 1). In [10], we need to take a union
bound for various estimates of the Green’s function over the upper half plane. A similar procedure would
have been impossible for small d. To overcome this difficulty, we restrict our estimates to the set Ω̄ of
“radius-R tree-like graphs”, which are graphs with benign local structures. In the high moments estimates
of the self-consistent equation (1.8), we also need to restrict the expectation to the set Ω̄. Here we used a key
property of the local resampling, namely, it preserves the radius-R tree like property with high probability.
In this way, we are able to show that for any d ⩾ 3 on the set Ω̄ key spectral properties hold with probability
1 − O(N −C ) for any C > 0.
     Random d-regular graphs can also be constructed from d copies of random perfect matchings, or random
lifts of a base graph containing two vertices and d edges between them. This class of random graphs obtained
from random lifts and in particular their extremal eigenvalues have been extensively studied [3, 4, 13, 40, 42,
60, 62, 70]. It is interesting to see if the approach in this paper can be used to analyze extremal eigenvalues
in this setting.
     In summary, the method in this paper, based on the self-consistent equation (1.7) and a new resampling
mechanism in Section 3, provides a new powerful method to analyze spectral properties of random d-regular
graphs up to the optimal d = 3. This method not only yields estimates on both eigenvalues and eigenvectors
over the entire range of spectrum, but its estimates on the location of the second eigenvalue (in fact, on
all extremal eigenvalues) are stronger than those obtained by the techniques of irreducible traces or non-
backtracking walks (which are sophisticated moment methods). In addition, we have isolated a class of


                                                        6
events beyond which all our estimates hold with very high probability. We believe that this method can
be further tighten to improve the error bound on the second largest eigenvalue and we hope that it will
eventually lead to the optimal bound λ2 , |λN | ⩽ 2 + N −c for all c < 2/3.

1.5    Some notations
We reserve letters in mathfrak mode, e.g. a, b, c, to represent universal constants, and use C, C1 , C2 , · · · , to
represent large universal constants, which may be different from line by line. We use letters in mathcal mode,
e.g. B, G, T , to represent graphs, or subgraphs, and letters in mathbb mode, e.g. B, G, T, to represent set of
vertices. We use letters in mathsf mode, e.g. A, B, U, W to represent index sets. For two quantities X and Y
depending on N , we write that X = O(Y ) if there exists some universal constant such that |X| ⩽ CY . We
write X = o(Y ), or X ≪ Y if the ratio |X|/Y → ∞ as N goes to infinity. We write X ≍ Y or X = Ω(Y ) if
there exists a universal constant C > 0 such that Y /C ⩽ |X| ⩽ CY . We remark that the implicit constants
may depend on d. We write a ∨ b = max{a, b} and a ∧ b = min{a, b}. We denote [[a, b]] = [a, b] ∩ Z and
[[N ]] = [[1, N ]]. We denote the complex upper half plane as C+ . We write a function g ≡ 0, if it is identically
zero, and g ̸≡ 0, if it is not identically zero.

2     Tree-like Graphs
In Section 2.1, we collect some basic definitions and terminology from graph theory, and recall some basic
structure properties of random d-regular graphs. In Section 2.2 and 2.3, we introduce the tree extension of
graphs and extension of graphs with general weights, which includes tree extension as a special case. We
also obtain estimates for the Green’s functions for those extended graphs.

2.1    Graphs
In this section we collect some definitions and terminology about graphs, and basic structure properties of
random d-regular graphs.

Graphs, adjacency matrices, Green’s functions Throughout this paper, graphs G are always simple
(i.e., have no self-loops or multiple edges) and have vertex degrees at most d (non-regular graphs are also
used). The degree of a vertex (the number of its adjacent vertices) in the graph G is denoted by degG (·).
The distance (length of the shortest path between two vertices) in the graph G is denoted by distG (·, ·).
The diameter diam(G) of the graph G is the greatest distance between any pair of vertices. If two vertices
i, j ∈ G are adjacent, i.e. distG (i, j) = 1, we write i ∼ j. For any graph G, the adjacency matrix is the
(possibly infinite) symmetric matrix A indexed by the vertices of the graph, with Aij = Aji = 1 if there is an
edge between i and√j, and Aij = 0 otherwise. Throughout the paper, we denote the normalized adjacency
matrix by H = A/ d − 1. Moreover, we denote the (unnormalized) adjacency matrix of a directed edge
(i, j) by eij , i.e. (eij )kl = δik δjl . The Green’s function of a graph G is the unique matrix G = G(G; z) defined
by G(H − z) = I for z ∈ C+ , where C+ is the upper half plane. The Stieltjes transform of the empirical
eigenvalue distribution of G is mN (z) = Tr G(z)/N . In Appendix A, several well-known properties of Green’s
function are summarized; they will be used throughout the paper. The Stieltjes transform mN (z) encodes
information of eigenvalues of H (and thus of A), and the Green’s function G(z) encodes both information of
eigenvalues and eigenvectors. In particular, the spectral resolution is given by η = Im[z]: the macroscopic
behavior corresponds to η of order 1, the mesoscopic behavior corresponds to 1/N ≪ η ≪ 1, and the
microscopic behavior of individual eigenvalues corresponds to η ≍ 1/N .

Subsets and Subgraphs Let G be a graph, and denote the set of its edges by the same symbol G and
its vertices by G. For any subset T ⊂ G, we define the graph G (T) from removing the vertices T and edges
adjacent to T from G. Then the adjacency matrix of G (T) is the restriction of that of G to G \ T. We write
G(T) for the Green’s function of G (T) . For any subgraph T ⊂ G, we denote by ∂T = {v ∈ G : distG (v, T ) = 1}
the vertex boundary of T in G, and by ∂E T = {e ∈ G : e is adjacent to T but e ̸∈ T } the edge boundary of
T in G.


                                                         7
Neighborhoods Given a subset T of the vertex set of a graph G and an integer r > 0, we denote the radius-
r neighborhood of T in G by Br (T, G), i.e., it is the subgraph induced by G on the set {j ∈ G : distG (T, j) ⩽ r}.
In particular, Br (i, G) is the radius-r neighborhood of the vertex i.

Trees The infinite d-regular tree is the unique (up to isometry) infinite connected d-regular graph without
cycles, and we denote it by X . The infinite (d − 1)-ary tree is the unique (up to isometry) infinite connected
tree that is d-regular at every vertex except for a distinguished root vertex o, which has degree d − 1, we
denote it by Y.

Excess of random d-regular graphs For any graph G, we define its excess to be the smallest number
of edges that must be removed to yield a graph with no cycles (a forest). It is given by

                   excess(G) := #edges(G) − #vertices(G) + #connected components(G).                         (2.1)

There are different conventions for the normalization of the excess. Our normalization is such that the excess
of a tree or forest is 0. Note that if H ⊂ G is a subgraph, then excess(H) ⩽ excess(G).
    We will use the following well-known estimates for the excess in random d-regular graphs. We recall the
constants 0 < c < 1 and R = (c/4) logd−1 N from the beginning of Section 1.2. The following proposition
implies that with high probability, random d-regular graphs are radius-R tree like as in Definition 1.1.

Proposition 2.1. Fix d ⩾ 3, let ω ⩾ 1 be an integer. If R ⩽ R, then the following holds for a uniformly
chosen random d-regular graph G on [[N ]], with probability at least 1 − O(N −(1−c)ω ) for N large enough we
have
   • All radius-R neighborhoods have excess at most ω:

                            for all i ∈ [[N ]], the subgraph BR (i, G) has excess at most ω.                 (2.2)


   • Most radius-R neighborhoods are trees:

                               |{i ∈ [[N ]] : the subgraph BR (i, G) is not a tree}| ⩽ N c .                 (2.3)

                                                                                               2c
Proof. We have that P((2.2) holds) = 1 − O(N −(1−c)ω ), and P((2.3) holds) = 1 − O(e−N ) from [10, Propo-
sition 4.1]. Proposition 2.1 follows from combining them.

Definition 2.3 (deficit function). Given a graph G with vertex set G and degrees bounded by d, a deficit
function for G is a function g : G → [[0, d]] satisfying degG (v) ⩽ d − g(v) for all vertices v ∈ G. We call a
vertex v ∈ G extensible if degG (v) < d − g(v).

Definition 2.4 (tree extenstion). Let G be a graph with deficit function g. The tree extension of G is the
(possibly infinite) graph TE(G) defined by attaching to any extensible vertex v in G, d − g(v) − degG (v) copies
of (d − 1)-ary trees. In this way, each vertex v ∈ G in the extended graph has degree d − g(v).

Remark 2.5. (Conventions for deficit functions) Throughout this paper, all graphs G are equipped with a
deficit function g. The interpretation of the deficit function g(v) is that it measures the difference to the
desired degree of the vertex v. We use the following conventions for deficit functions.
   • For d-regular graphs G ∈ GN,d and the infinite d-regular graph X , their deficit function is identically
     zero, i.e. g ≡ 0. For the infinite (d − 1)-ary tree Y, its deficit function g(o) = 1 at the root vertex, and
     g = 0 elsewhere.
   • If T is a subset of the vertices of G, and g is the deficit function of G, then the deficit function g ′ of
     G (T) is given by

                                           g ′ (v) = g + degG (v) − degG (T) (v).                           (2.8)

      Thus TE(G (T) ) can be obtained from TE(G) by removing the edges incident to T.
   • If H ⊂ G is a subgraph (which was not obtained as G (T) ), then the deficit function of H is given by the
     restriction of the deficit function of G on H, unless specified explicitly.
   For each d ⩾ 3, we associate an integer ωd > 0 so that an infinite d-regular graph with excess less or
equal than ωd has good spectral properties:


                                                           9
Definition 2.6 (ωd definition). Fix d ⩾ 3, we define ωd to be the largest ω such that the following holds:

Remark 2.7. For d = 3 we have ω3 = 1, see Proposition A.2. In general, ωd ⩾ 1 is an increasing function
of d, and ωd goes to infinity as d increases.

Remark 2.8. Thanks to the resolvent identity (A.4), if a graph G satisfies (2.9) and (2.10), it still satisfies
(2.9) after removing one vertex. But (2.10) might fail. In fact, after removing vertex j from G, the deficit
function of the new graph G (j) is as given in (2.8). And the Green’s function of its tree extension satisfies

2.3    Green’s function for extension of graphs with weight ∆(z)

corresponds to the extension of graphs with the weight given by the Stieltjes transform of the semicircle
distribution msc (z). By a perturbative argument, then we obtain estimates for the Green’s function for
extensions of graphs with general weight ∆(z) close to msc (z).

3     Local Resampling
In this section, we introduce the local resampling of a random d-regular graph, which is a modification of
the local resampling in [10, Section 7]. The procedure effectively resamples the edges on the boundary of
balls of radius ℓ, by switching them with random edges from the remainder of the graph. This resampling
generalizes the local resampling introduced in [11], where switchings were used to resample the neighbors of


                                                            12
a vertex (corresponding to ℓ = 0). The local resampling provides an effective access to the randomness of
the random d-regular graph, which is fundamental for the proof of Theorem 4.2.
    To introduce the local resampling, we require some definitions. We consider simple d-regular graphs on
vertex set [[N ]] and identify such graphs with their sets of edges throughout this section. (Deficit functions
do not play a role in this section.) For any graph G, we denote the set of unoriented edges by E(G), and the
set of oriented edges by ⃗                                                       ⃗⊂ ⃗
                          E(G) := {(u, v), (v, u) : {u, v} ∈ E(G)}. For a subset S  E(G), we denote by S the
set of corresponding non-oriented edges. For a subset S ⊂ E(G) of edges we denote by [S] ⊂ [[N ]] the set of
vertices incident to any edge in S. Moreover, for a subset V ⊂ [[N ]] of vertices, we define E(G)|V to be the
subgraph of G induced on V.

                                                        v1                           v2



                                                        v3                           v4

      Our local resampling involves a fixed center vertex, we now assume to be vertex o, and a radius ℓ. Given
a d-regular graph G, we abbreviate T = Bℓ (o, G) (which may not be a tree) and its vertex set T. The
edge boundary ∂E T of T consists of the edges in G with one vertex in T and the other vertex in [[N ]] \ T.
We enumerate ∂E T as ∂E T = {e1 , e2 , . . . , eµ }, where eα = {lα , aα } with lα ∈ T and aα ∈ [[N ]] \ T. We
orient the edges ei by defining ⃗ei = (lα , aα ). We notice that µ and the edges e1 , e2 , . . . , eµ depend on G.
The edges eα are distinct, but the vertices aα are not necessarily distinct and neither are the vertices lα .
Our local resampling switches the edge boundary of T with randomly chosen edges in G (T) if the switching
is admissible (see below), and leaves them in place otherwise. To perform our local resampling, we choose
(b1 , c1 ), . . . , (bµ , cµ ) to be independent, uniformly chosen oriented edges from the graph G (T) , i.e., the oriented
edges of G that are not incident to T, and define

                                      ⃗α = {⃗eα , (bα , cα )},

data (b1 , c1 ), (b2 , c2 ), · · · , (bµ , cµ ).

                                                                         13
    We remark, the indicator functions Iα and Jα are different from those in [10, Section 7]. Our indicator
function Iα imposes a “tree” condition, which ensures that aα and {bα , cα } are far away from each other,
and their neighborhoods are trees. And our indicator function Jα imposes an “isolation” condition, which
ensures that we only perform simple switching when the switching pair is far away from other switching
pairs. In this way, we do not need to keep track of the interaction between different simple switchings.
Notice that all conditions related to Iα and Jα are imposed on balls of radius R/4.

i.e., the set of pairs of oriented edges in E      ⃗ containing ⃗eα and another oriented edge in G (T) . Therefore
       ⃗  ⃗            ⃗
S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sµ ) is uniformly distributed over the set S(G) = S1 (G) × · · · × Sµ (G).

Definition 3.2. For any graph G ∈ GN,d , denote by ι(G) = {G} × S(G) the fibre of local resamplings of G
(with respect to vertex o), and define the enlarged probability space
                                                             G
                                         G̃N,d = ι(GN,d ) =       ι(G),
                                                                    G∈GN,d


with the probability measure P̃(G, S) := P(G)PG (S) = (1/|GN,d |)(1/|S(G)|) for any (G, S) ∈ G̃N,d . Here
P(G) = 1/|GN,d | is the uniform probability measure on GN,d , and PG is the uniform probability measure on
S(G).
    Let π : G̃N,d → GN,d , (G, S) 7→ G be the canonical projection onto the first component. It is easy to see
that π is measure preserving: P = P̃ ◦ π −1 .
    On the enlarged probability space, we define the maps

                                 T̃ : G̃N,d → G̃N,d , T̃ (G, S) := (TS (G), T (S)),                                       (3.5)
                                 T : G̃N,d → GN,d ,     T (G, S) := π(T̃ (G, S)) = TS (G).                                (3.6)

For the statement of the next proposition, recall that GN,d denotes the set of simple d-regular graph on [[N ]].
For any finite graph T on a subset of [[N ]], we define GN,d (T ) := {G ∈ GN,d : Bℓ (o, G) = T } to be the set of
d-regular graphs whose radius-ℓ neighborhood of the vertex o in G is T .

Proposition 3.3. For any graph T , we have

                                              T̃ (ι(GN,d (T ))) = ι(GN,d (T )),                                           (3.7)

and T̃ is an involution: T̃ ◦ T̃ = id.

Proof. Since our local resampling does not change the radius ℓ neighborhood of o, from our construction,
T̃ (ι(GN,d (T ))) ⊂ ι(GN,d (T )). Next we show that T̃ is an involution, then it follows T̃ (ι(GN,d (T ))) =
ι(GN,d (T )).
    To verify that T̃ is an involution, let (G, S) ∈ G̃N,d and abbreviate (G̃, S̃) = T̃ (G, S). Then, thanks to
(3.7), the edge boundaries of the radius-ℓ neighborhoods of o have the same number of edges µ in G̃ and G.
Moreover, we can choose the enumeration of the boundary of the ℓ-ball in G̃ such that, for any α ∈ [[1, µ]],
we have Tα (S⃗α ) ∈ Sα (G̃). Define

                                      W̃S̃ := {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : Iα (G̃, S̃)Jα (G̃, S̃) = 1}.

We claim that W̃S̃ = WS .
     First, by the definition of switchings, it is easy to see that Jα (G̃, S̃) = Jα (G, S). It suffices to verify
that Iα (G̃, S̃) = Iα (G, S) also holds for all α ∈ [[1, µ]]. If Jα (G̃, S̃) = Jα (G, S) = 1 and Iα (G, S) = 1, then
{aβ , bβ , cβ } ̸∈ BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G (T) ) for all β ̸= α. Moreover, α is switchable and BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G (T) ) ∪
{bα , cα } = BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G̃ (T) ) ∪ {aα , bα } have zero excess. This implies Iα (G̃, S̃) = 1. Otherwise if


                                                              14
Jα (G̃, S̃) = Jα (G, S) = 0, or Iα (G, S) = 0 then index α is not switchable. Therefore we have that the
R/4−neighborhoods of {aα , bα , cα } never change, i.e. BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G (T) ) = BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G̃ (T) ). In
this case, we also have Iα (G̃, S̃) = Iα (G, S). In summary, we have verified the claim W̃S̃ = WS . By the
definition of our switchings, it follows that T (S̃) = S and TS̃ (G̃) = G. Therefore T̃ is an involution.

Remark 3.4. We remark that from the proof of Proposition 3.3, the radius R/4 neighborhoods of the vertex
o in the original graph G and in the switched graph G̃ are isomorphic to each other.

Proposition 3.5. T̃ and T are measure preserving: P̃ ◦ T̃ −1 = P̃ and P̃ ◦ T −1 = P.
   In other words, that T is measure preserving means that if G is uniform over GN,d , and given G, we
choose S uniform over S(G), then TS (G) is uniform over GN,d .

Proof. We decompose the enlarged probability space according to the radius-ℓ neighborhood of o as
                                 [
                         G̃N,d =    G̃N,d (T ), where G̃N,d (T ) = ι(GN,d (T )).                                      (3.8)
                                         T

Notice that, given any T , the size of the set S(G) is (by construction) independent of the graph G ∈ GN,d (T ).
Therefore, given any T , the restricted measure P̃|G̃N,d (T ) is uniform, i.e., proportional to the counting
measure on the finite set G̃N,d (T ). Since, by Proposition 3.3, the map T̃ is an involution on G̃N,d (T ), it
is in particular a bijection and preserves the uniform measure P̃|G̃N,d (T ). Since T̃ acts diagonally in the
decomposition (3.8), this implies that the map T̃ preserves the measure P̃. Since P = P̃ ◦ π −1 and T = π ◦ T̃ ,
it immediately follows that also T is measure preserving:
                                      P̃ ◦ T −1 = P̃ ◦ T̃ −1 ◦ π −1 = P̃ ◦ π −1 = P,
as claimed.


4      Weak Local Law and Delocalization of Eigenvectors
In this section we prove the following theorem. It states that with high probability the Green’s function of a
random d-regular graph G at vertices i, j, can be approximated by the Green’s function of the tree extension
of a radius-r neighborhood Br (i, j, G) of vertices i, j. The delocalization of eigenvectors Theorem 1.4 follows
from Theorem 4.2 by a standard argument [27, Section 18.5].
      Before stating Theorem 4.2, we need to introduce some parameters

Definition 4.1 (Choices of parameters). We fix parameters 0 < c < 1, a ⩾ 12, b ⩾ 25a, 0 < r ⩽ c/32 and
a large constant q. Let R = (c/4) logd−1 N , r = r logd−1 N and ℓ ∈ [[a logd−1 log N, 2a logd−1 log N ]]. We
restrict ourself to the spectral domain z ∈ C+ with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N . With this choice of parameters,
                  R/8 ⩾ r = O(log N ) ≫ ℓ = O(log log N ),          (log N )a ⩽ (d − 1)ℓ ⩽ (log N )2a .               (4.1)

    For z = E(z) + iη(z) ∈ C+ , we define κ(z) = min{|E(z) − 2|, |E(z) + 2|}, the distance from z to the
spectral edges ±2. From the quadratic equation of msc (z), we have

Theorem 4.2. Fix d ⩾ 3, 0 < c < 1, R = (c/4) logd−1 N and recall the set of radius-R tree like graphs
Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition (1.1). For any large C > 0 and N large enough, with probability 1 − O(N −C ) with
respect to the uniform measure on Ω̄, the Green’s function of G ∈ Ω̄ satisfies

                                                                                 ε(z)
                     |Gij (G, z) − Gij (Ext(Br (i, j, G), msc (z)), z)| ≲ p                   ,              (4.6)
                                                                           κ(z) + η(z) + ε(z)

for any vertices i, j ∈ [[N ]], and the Stieltjes transform of its empirical eigenvalues satisfies

                                                                       ε(z)
                                   |mN (z) − md (z)| ≲ p                                ,                    (4.7)
                                                                  κ(z) + η(z) + ε(z)

uniformly for z ∈ C+ , with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N .
    We remark that while Theorem 4.2 shows that the spectral density (or its Stieltjes transform, which is
the trace of the Green’s function) does concentrate around the Kesten-Mckay law. The individual entries of
the Green’s function of the random d-regular graph with bounded degree is approximated by the Green’s
function of a neighborhood of these entries. Recall that the off-diagonal entries of the Green’s function
of a Wigner matrix is uniformly small. This property clearly fails for the Green’s function of the random
d-regular graph with a fixed degree.
   In Section 4.2, we give the proof of Theorem 4.2, which uses Propositions 4.6 and 4.8 as input. The
proofs of Propositions 4.6 and 4.8 are given in Sections 5 and 6 respectively.

4.1    Notations and Definitions
To study the change of graphs before and after local resampling, we define the following graphs (which are
not d-regular).
   • G is the original unswitched graph;
   • G̃ is the switched graph TS (G) ;
   • G (T) is the unswitched graph obtained from G with vertices T removed;
   • G̃ (T) is the switched graph obtained from G̃ with vertices T removed;
   • G (TWS ) = G̃ (TWS ) is the intermediate graph obtained from G (T) with vertices WS = {bα : α ∈ WS }
     removed, or equivalently obtained from G̃ (T) with vertices WS removed.
Following the conventions of Remark 2.5, the deficit functions of these graphs are given by (2.8). More
explicitly, the deficit functions are simply g(v) = d − deg(v). We abbreviate their Green’s functions by G,
G(T) , G̃(TWS ) = G(TWS ) , G̃(T) , and G̃ respectively. The local resampling as defined in Section 3 has a smaller
effect in G (T) than they do in G. Indeed, in the original graph G, simple switchings have the effect of removing


                                                             16
two edges and adding two edges, while in G (T) simple switchings only remove the edges {bα , cα }α∈WS and
add the edges {aα , bα }α∈WS .
    The small distance behavior is captured in terms of cycles in neighborhoods of radius O(R). For any
graph, we recall that excess is the smallest number of edges that must be removed to yield a graph with no
cycles (a forest). We also recall the set of radius-R tree like graphs Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition (1.1). We will
also need the following set Ω̄+ . Roughly speaking, for any fixed large constant q, from a d-regular graph in
G ∈ Ω̄, with probability 1 − O(N −q ) with respect to the randomness of the resampling data S, the switched
graph TS (G) ∈ Ω̄+ . The d-reguar graphs in Ω̄+ are also tree-like at small distances. However, compared
with d-regular graphs in Ω̄, they are a bit more deviated from trees locally, depending on q.

Definition 4.3. We define the set Ω̄+ ⊂ GN,d to consist of graphs such that
   • the radius-R/8 neighborhood of any vertex has excess at most ωd , the radius-R/2 neighborhood of any
     vertex has excess at most Cq , where Cq is a constant depending on q and explicitly specified in the proof
     of Proposition 5.12.
   • the number of vertices that have a radius-R neighborhood that contains a cycle is at most 2N c .
     Notice that the sets Ω̄ and Ω̄+ are subsets of GN,d defined by deterministic properties. Moreover, it is
clearly that Ω̄ ⊂ Ω̄+ . For any graph in Ω̄, Ω̄+ , radius-R/8 neighborhood of any vertex has excess at most
ωd . The Green’s functions of their extensions with weight close to msc (z) are stable, and satisfy Proposition
2.12. However, the small distance graphic behavior captured by sets Ω̄, Ω̄+ does no guarantee nice spectral
properties, i.e. stability of their Green’s function. We need the following notion of spectral regular graphs.
For any finite d-regular graph G, we introduce the following quantity
                                                        1 X X (j)
                                          Q(G, z) =               Gii (G, z),                                (4.8)
                                                       Nd   j:i∼j
                                                          i∈[[N ]]


where G(j) (G, z) is the Green’s function of the graph obtained from G by removing the vertex j. We define
the following set of d-regular graphs, whose Green’s function has good estimates.

Definition 4.4. For z ∈ C+ , we define the set of spectral regular graphs, Ω(z) ⊂ Ω̄ , to be the set of graphs
G such that
                                                                     ε(z)
                                 |Q(G, z) − msc (z)| ⩽ p                          ,
                                                             κ(z) + η(z) + ε(z)                              (4.9)
                                 |Gij (z) − Gij (Ext(Br (i, j, G), Q(G, z)), z)| ⩽ ε(z),

for any two vertices i, j ∈ [[N ]].
    Notice that the last inequality in particular holds for i = j and thus the error for the diagonal terms
                                    ε(z)
of Green’s function is ε(z) ≪ √               . In other words, we are able to approximate Green’s function
                                      κ(z)+η(z)+ε(z)
of the original graph with that of a tree like graph provide that extension uses the weight Q(G, z). A key
novelty of this paper is to derive a self-consistent equation for the quantity Q(G, z): Q(G, z) ≈ Yℓ (Q(G, z), z),
where the function Yℓ (·, z) is defined in (2.14). The self-consistent equation captures the square root behavior
at the spectral edge of the empirical density. This is reminiscent of the quadratic equation for the Stieltjes
transform of semicircle distribution. This enables us to improve the results in [10] to the spectral edge.
    We have so far defined three subsets of GN,d : Ω(z) ⊂ Ω̄ ⊂ Ω̄+ . The set Ω̄+ consists of graphs with
regularity conditions weaker than those of Ω̄. And the spectral regular graph set Ω(z), consists of d-regular
graphs satisfying certain resolvent estimates.

4.2    Two key Propositions
The key inputs to prove Theorem 4.2 are the following two Propositions: Proposition 4.6 states for G ∈ Ω(z),
with high probability with respect to the resampling data S, we have good estimates for the Green’s function
of the switched graphs G̃ = TS (G) and G̃ (T) . We denote the set of such graphs by Ω+o (z). Proposition 4.8
states for G ∈ Ω+o (z), with high probability with respect to the resampling data S, the Green’s function
of the switched graph G̃ = TS (G) has an improved estimate near vertex o. For the rest of this section, all

statements about local resampling refer to a neighborhood centered at a vertex labelled by o. The proofs of
Propositions 4.6 and 4.8 will occupy Sections 5 and 6.

4.3    Proof of Theorem 4.2
Assuming the propositions in the previous subsection, Theorem 4.2 is an easy consequence of the following
proposition. Instead of approximating the Green’s function Gij by the Green’s function of the tree extension
of a neighborhood Br (i, j, G) of vertices i, j, we approximate Gij by the extension of Br (i, j, G) with the
weight Q(G, z). The approximation with Q(G, z) as the boundary weight, leads naturally to the quadratic
self-consistent equation of Q(G, z), which can be used to obtain improved estimates of Q(G, z) for both the
bulk and edge regions.

Proposition 4.9. Fix d ⩾ 3, 0 < c < 1, R = (c/4) logd−1 N and recall the set of radius-R tree like graphs
Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition (1.1). For any large C > 0 and N large enough, with probability 1 − O(N −C ) with
respect to the uniform measure on Ω̄, the quantity Q(G, z) as defined in (4.8) satisfies
                                                                          ε(z)

and the Green’s function satisfies
                                 |Gij (z) − Gij (Ext(Br (i, j, G), Q(G, z)), z)| ⩽ ε(z),                    (4.18)

uniformly in i, j ∈ [[N ]], and z = E(z) + iη(z) ∈ C+ , with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N .

then Br (i, j, G) has excess at most ωd , Proposition 2.12 and (4.17) imply

    In Proposition 4.11, we prove that for |z| ⩾ 2d the event Ω− (z) holds deterministically. Since the Green’s
functions Gij (z) are Lipschitz in z, conditioning on the event Ω− (w), Ω(z) holds deterministically for all {z ∈
C+ : |z − w| ≲ 1/N, Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N }. In Proposition 4.12, using Propositions 4.6 and 4.8, we show that
the difference of the two sets Ω(z), Ω− (z) is negligible, i.e. P (Ω(z) \ Ω− (z)) = O(N −q+1 ). As a consequence,
on the event Ω− (z), by losing a small probability, the event Ω− (z − i/N ) holds. Starting from |z| ⩾ 2d, where
Ω− (z) holds deterministically, it follows by an iterative scheme, with high probability, the events Ω− (z) holds
for all z in the form (−2d + α/N ) + i (2d − β/N ) with α ∈ [[0, 4dN ]] and β ∈ [[0, 2dN − (log N )b ]]. Using
again the Green’s functions Gij (z) are Lipschitz in z, this implies (4.19), and Proposition 4.9 follows.

                                                                23
5       Proof of Proposition 4.6: Stability Under Local Resampling
In this Section, mostly by perturbation arguments and Schur complement formulas, we prove Proposition
4.6: if the Green’s functions of G or G (T) satisfy certain estimates, the Green’s functions of the switched
graphs, i.e. G̃ or G̃ (T) , satisfy similar (slightly worse) estimates. In Section 5.1, we obtain the estimates for
the Green’s function of G (T) . In Sections 5.2–5.4, we obtain the estimates for the Green’s functions of G (TWS )
and G̃ (T) . In Section 5.5, we obtain the estimates for the Green’s functions of G̃. In Section 5.6, we show
that the difference between Q(G, z) by Q(G̃, z) is small. The proof of Proposition 4.6 is given in Section 5.7.
We will outline the major steps and ideas of the proofs in Remarks 5.8 and 5.19.

5.1     Stability under removal of a neighborhood
We recall the notations and definitions from Section 3 and Section 4. Our local resampling involves a fixed
center vertex o, and a radius ℓ. Given a d-regular graph G, we abbreviate T = Bℓ (o, G) (which may not be a
tree) and its vertex set T. We enumerate edge boundary ∂E T as ∂E T = {e1 , e2 , . . . , eµ }, where eα = {lα , aα }
with lα ∈ T and aα ∈ [[N ]] \ T. The following deterministic estimate shows that removing the neighborhood
T, which is the vertex set of Bℓ (o, G), from the graph G has a small effect on the Green’s function in the
complement of T.

Proposition 5.1. Fix d ⩾ 3, ωd as in Definition 2.6, and z ∈ C+ with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N . Let G ∈ Ω(z)
be a spectral regular graph, as defined in Definition 4.4. Then, for all vertices i, j ∈ [[N ]] \ T, we have
                           (T)
                         |Gij (z) − Gij (Ext(Br (i, j, G (T) ), Q(G, z)), z)| ≲ (log N )3 ε(z).               (5.1)
                                          +
Especially, we have Ω(z) ⊂ Ω+
                            o (z), where Ωo (z) is defined in Definition 4.5.


5.1.1    Boundary of a neighborhood
Proposition 5.2. Let G be a d-regular graph on [[N ]] and fix R ⩾ 2ℓ. Assume that B = BR (o, G) has excess
at most ω. Then the following hold.
    • After removing T, most boundary vertices of T are far away from the other boundary vertices:

                            |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : ∃β ∈ [[1, µ]] \ {α}, distG (T) (aα , aβ ) ⩽ R/2}| ⩽ 2ω.          (5.2)

    • After removing T, any vertex x ∈ [[N ]] \ T can only be close to at most ω + 1 boundary vertices of T:

                                     |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (x, aα ) ⩽ R/2}| ⩽ ω + 1.                     (5.3)

   Next, for R ⩾ 2ℓ, we have the following deterministic bound on the deficit functions for the connected
components of the subgraph obtained from B = BR (o, G) by removing T.

Proposition 5.3. Under the same assumption as in Proposition 5.2, the following holds: Let A be the
annulus obtained by removing T from B, i.e. A = B (T) , then for any connected component of A, its deficit
function is nonzero and the sum of its deficit function plus its excess is at most ω + 1.
    For the above statements, recall that we view B as subgraphs of G (which has zero deficit function), the
deficit function of B is also zero. By our conventions in Remark 2.5, the deficit function of A = B (T) is given
by (2.8). In the remainder of this section, we prove Propositions 5.2 and 5.3.
    By assumption the neighborhood B has excess at most ω. We partition [[1, µ]] into sets {A1 , A2 , · · · , Aκ },
such that α and β are in the same set As iff aα and aβ are in the same connected component of A.
                                                                          Pκ

Remark 5.8 (Remark on Methods). The decomposition (5.6) and the Schur complement formulas in this
case (5.7) and (5.8) are the main tool in this paper. These formulas provide estimates on G(T) and P (T)
based on informations of G and P . In order to use the Schur complement formulas (5.7) and (5.8), we need
to estimate G|−1
              T . Since G|T can be approximate locally by P |T , taking the inverse can be easily achieved by
the standard resolvent identity since T does not contain too many vertices.

5.2    Graph distance between switched vertices
We recall the notations and definitions from Section 3 and Section 4. In this section, we give some basic
estimates for the local resampling and graph distance between switched vertices.

Proposition 5.9. For any vertex x ∈ [[N ]] \ T, and α ∈ [[1, µ]]

                                                PG (bα = x) = PG (cα = x) ⩽ 2/N,                                                  (5.19)

where PG (·) is defined in Definition 3.2.


                                                                     27
Proof. We recall that, for any α ∈ [[1, µ]], the oriented edge (bα , cα ) is uniformly chosen from the oriented
edges of G (T) . By the definition of T, there are N d − O((d − 1)ℓ ) oriented edges in G (T) , and since for any
vertex x ∈ G (T) , the degree obeys degG (T) (x) ⩽ d,
                                                                         d            2
                               PG (bα = x) = PG (cα = x) ⩽                           ⩽ .
                                                                  N d − O((d − 1)ℓ )  N



   We remark that the edges {bα , cα } are randomly chosen in the graph G (T) . In the following proposition,
we show that, by paying a small probability, we can make sure that most {bα , cα } are at least distance R
away from any x ∈ [[N ]] \ T, and their radius R neighborhoods are trees.

Proposition 5.10. For any graph G ∈ Ω̄ or Ω̄+ (as in Definitions 1.1 and 4.3), and any large number
q > 0, the following holds with PG -probability at least 1 − O(N −q ):
   • Any vertex x ∈ [[N ]] \ T is far away from most vertices in the set {a1 , b1 , c1 , . . . , aµ , bµ , cµ }:
                                 |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/4}| = Oq (1).                   (5.20)

   • Most indices α ∈ [[1, µ]] are good in the following sense:
           |B1 | = Oq (1), with B1 = {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (aα , {bα , cα }) ⩽ R},                               (5.21)
           |B2 | = Oq (1), with B2 = {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : ∃β ̸= α, distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩽ R/4},   (5.22)
                                                                         (T)
           |B3 | = Oq (1), with B3 = {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : BR ({bα , cα }, G         ) is not a tree}.                     (5.23)

Proof. For (5.20), by taking R = R/2 in (5.3), we have
                                   |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (x, aα ) ⩽ R/4}| = Oq (1).                             (5.24)
We notice that in any graph with degree bounded by d, the number of vertices at distance at most R from
vertex x is bounded by O((d − 1)R ). By (5.19) we have
                                                                               (d − 1)R
                                      PG (distG (T) (x, {bα , cα }) ⩽ R) ≲              .

      The estimates (5.20) and (5.22) also imply the following estimates for the switched graph G̃ (T) .

Proposition 5.11. For any graph G ∈ Ω̄ or Ω̄+ (as in Definitions 1.1 and 4.3), assume (5.20) and (5.22),
then
   • For any index α ∈ [[1, µ]] \ B2 (as defined in (5.22)),

                               distG̃ (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ : β ∈ [[1, µ]] \ {α}}) > R/4.            (5.28)

   • For any vertex x ∈ [[N ]] \ T,

                                 |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG̃ (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/4}| = Oq (1).                (5.29)

Proof. For our local resampling, it holds that if distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ : β ∈ [[1, µ]] \ {α}}) >
R/4, then distG̃ (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ : β ∈ [[1, µ]] \ {α}}) > R/4. The first claim (5.28) follows
from the definition of the set B2 , as in (5.22). If distG̃ (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/4, then either α ∈ B2 or
distG (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/4. Thus (5.29) follows from (5.20) and (5.22).



Proposition 5.12. For any graph G ∈ Ω̄ (as in Definition 1.1), there exists event F1 (G) ⊂ S(G) with
probability PG (F1 (G)) = 1 − O(N −q ), it holds that for any switching data S ∈ S(G), G̃ = TS (G) ∈ Ω̄+ as
defined in Definition 4.3.

Proof. We denote F1 (G) ⊂ S(G) the set of switching data S ∈ S(G) such that the following statements hold

                        |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/2}| ≲ q,         x ∈ [[N ]] \ T,       (5.30)

and
                         |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : ∃β ̸= α, distG (T) (aα , {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩽ R/2}| ≲ q,
                         |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : ∃β ̸= α, distG (T) ({bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩽ R}| ≲ q,              (5.31)
                                                              (T)
                         |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : BR ({bα , cα }, G         ) is not a tree}| ≲ q.

The same argument as for (5.20), (5.22) and (5.23), by using Proposition 5.2 with R = R, implies that
PG (F1 (G)) = 1 − O(N −q ). In the following, we prove that for any S ∈ F1 (G), G̃ = TS (G) ∈ Ω̄+ .
    We recall the local resampling from Section 3, that G̃ is obtained from G by removing those edges
{aα , lα }, {bα , cα }, and adding edges {lα , cα }, {aα , bα } for α ∈ WS . If a vertex x is far away from those vertices
involving in local resampling, i.e. distG (x, {lα , aα , bα , cα : α ∈ WS }) ⩾ R, then its radius R neighborhood
does not change BR (x, G) = BR (x, G̃). Especially if BR (x, G) is a truncated d-regular tree, so is BR (x, G̃).
Therefore the number of vertices that have a radius-R neighborhood that contains a cycle in G̃ is at most
N c + 4|WS |(d − 1)R ⩽ 2N c .
    If x ̸∈ T or x ̸∈ BR/8 ({aα : α ∈ [[1, µ]]\WS }∪{aα , bα , cα : α ∈ WS }, G̃ (T) ), the radius R/8 neighborhood of
x in G and G̃ are the same. Thus the excess of BR/8 (x, G) is the same as that of BR/8 (x, G̃), which is at most ωd .
By the definition of switchable set WS as in (3.2), if α ∈ WS , then distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩾ R/4
for all β ∈ [[1, µ]] \ {α} and the subgraph BR/4 ({aα , bα , cα }, G (T) ) after adding the edge {aα , bα } is a tree. If
x ∈ T or x ∈ BR/8 ({aα : α ∈ [[1, µ]]\WS }∪{aα , bα , cα : α ∈ WS }, G̃ (T) ), the neighborhood BR/8 (x, G̃) is either
a subgraph of BR/4 ({aα , bα }) for some α ∈ WS , or a subgraph of BR/4 ({aα : α ∈ [[1, µ]] \ WS }, G \ {{lα , aα } :
α ∈ WS }) attaching some subtrees at {cα : α ∈ WS }. In both cases the excess is at most ωd .
    If x ∈ T, let B ⊂ WS be the set of indices in (5.31); If x ∈ [[N ]] \ T, let B ⊂ WS be the set of indices
in (5.31), union with {α ∈ WS : distG̃ (T) (x, {aα , bα , cα }) ⩽ R/2}. From our choice of F1 (G), it holds that
|B| ≲ q. The neighborhood BR/2 (x, G̃) is a subgraph of

               BR/2 ({x} ∪ {aα , bα , cα : α ∈ B} ∪ {aα : α ∈ [[1, µ]] \ WS }, G \ {{lα , aα } : α ∈ WS })         (5.32)

adding the edges {{aα , bα } : α ∈ B}, removing the edges {{bα , cα } : α ∈ B}, and attaching subtrees at
{cα , α ∈ WS \ B}. Therefore the excess of BR/2 (x, G̃) is upper bounded by |B| = O(q) plus the excess of the

                                                                29
graph (5.32). By our assumption, G ∈ Ω̄, each radius R neighborhood of G contains at most ωd cycles. So is
the graph G \ {{lα , aα } : α ∈ WS }. The graph (5.32) is an union of 1 + 3|B| + (µ − |WS |) = O(q) radius R/2
neighborhoods. Its excess is at most Oq (1). We conclude that the excess of BR/2 (x, G̃) is at most Oq (1).
This finishes the proof of Proposition 5.12.



5.3     The Green’s function distance and switching cells
The bounds proved in Section 5.2 provide accurate control for distances at most O(R). However, random
vertices are typically much further away from each other, and as mentioned in Section 4.2, we require
stronger upper bounds on the Green’s function for such large distances. These bounds are in fact a general
consequence of the Ward identity,
                                       X               Im[Gii (z)]
                                          |Gij (z)|2 =             ,                                (5.33)
                                        j
                                                         Im[z]

which holds for the Green’s function of any symmetric matrix (see (A.5)). To make use of it, we introduce
a much coarser measure of distance in terms of the size of the Green’s function as follows.

5.3.1   Definition
We recall the control parameter φ(z) from (4.4), and the admissible set WS ∈ [[1, µ]] from (3.2). We define
a relation ≃ on [[N ]] \ T by setting x ≃ y if and only if distG (T) (x, y) ⩽ R/4 or there exist vertices u, v with
distG (T) (u, x) ⩽ 1, distG (T) (y, v) ⩽ 1, and
                                                |G(T)
                                                  uv (z)| ⩾ φ(z).                                             (5.34)
We will say x, y are Green’s function correlated at the threshold φ(z) if x ≃ y. For two sets X, Y ⊂ [[N ]] \ T,
we write X ≃ Y if there exist x ∈ X and y ∈ Y such that x ≃ y. Otherwise, we write X ̸≃ Y.
    The concept of Green’s function correlated is an important tool introduced in [10]. The rationale is that
the Ward identity (5.33) provides only an L2 bound on the Green’s function. This bound is not strong enough
since some entires Gij can be large. These special entries needed to be treated with additional arguments
which explore in particular the fact that the resampling data are chosen uniformly and independently. We
recall the index set WS from (3.2), for α ̸= β ∈ WS we have distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) > R/4.

Definition 5.13 (Cells). We denote Cr0 the set

                                   Cr0 = {α ∈ WS : ∃β ∈ [[1, µ]] : {bα , cα } ≃ aβ }.

The relation ≃ and graph distance induce a relation on the set WS \ Cr0 . We write α ≈ β if and only if
either {bα , cα } ≃ {bβ , cβ }. The relation ≈ induces a partition of the set WS \ Cr0 = Cr1 ∪ Cr2 ∪ · · · Crκ . We
define sets S0 , S1 , . . . , Sκ ⊂ [[N ]] \ T called cells by

               S0 = {a1 , a2 , · · · , aµ } ∪α∈Cr0 {bα , cα },    Ss = ∪α∈Crs {bα , cα },   s = 1, 2, · · · , κ.   (5.35)

    For later use, we note the following elementary properties of cells:
                                                                                            (T)
   • For any x ∈ B1 (Ss , G (T) ) and y ∈ B1 (St , G (T) ) such that s ̸= t we have |Gxy (z)| < φ(z). For any vertex
                                                                       (T)
     x ∈ [[N ]] \ T, if x ̸≃ Ss , then for any y ∈ B1 (Ss , G (T) ), |Gxy (z)| < φ(z).
   • The cells are far away from each other, for any s ̸= t we have distG (T) (Ss , St ) ⩾ R/4.

5.3.2   Estimates
The next proposition shows that the cells do not cluster.

Proposition 5.14. Let G ∈ Ω(z) or Ω+     o (z) as defined in Definitions 4.4 and 4.5 respectively. Then for
any large number q, with probabililty at least 1 − O(N −q ) under PG (·) (as in Definition 3.2), the following
estimates hold:


                                                                 30
   • Any x ∈ [[N ]] \ T is ≃-connected to fewer than O(log N ) of vertices in {b1 , c1 , b2 , c2 , . . . , bµ , cµ },

                                              |{α ∈ [[1, µ]] : x ≃ {bα , cα }}| ≲ log N.                                 (5.36)

       In particular, x is ≃-connected to O(log N ) of the cells.
   • Most cells are in the form {bα , cα }

                                      |{α ∈ WS : α ∈ Cr0 or α ∈ Crs , |Crs | > 1}| ≲ log N.                              (5.37)

       In particular, each cell contains at most O(log N ) of {bα , cα : α ∈ WS }.
    In the remainder of this section, we prove the above proposition. It is essentially a straightforward
consequence of the definitions, combined with union bounds.

                                                    |Vx | ≲ N/(log N )4a .                                               (5.42)

        PG ({bα , cα } ≃ x) ⩽ PG (distG (T) (x, {bα , cα }) ⩽ R/4) + PG (bα or cα ∈ B1 (∪u∈B1 (x,G (T) ) Vu , G (T) ))
                            ≲ (d − 1)R/4 /N + d2 |Vx |/N ≲ (d − 1)R/4 /N + d2 /(log N )4a ≲ 1/(log N )4a ,

where the second inequality holds because bα , cα are approximately uniform (5.19).

Proof of Proposition 5.14. The proof of (5.36) is similar to that of (5.20). From our construction of local
resampling, {bα , cα } ≃ x is an independent event for different α. By the union bound and (5.38), we have

    For (5.37), we recall the index sets B1 , B2 ⊂ [[1, µ]] from (5.21), (5.22):

                       B1 = {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : distG (T) (aα , {bα , cα }) ⩽ R},
                       B2 = {α ∈ [[1, µ]] : ∃β ̸= α, distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩽ R/4}.

    From the discussion above, only α ∈ B1 ∪ B2 or {bα , cα } ≃ {aβ : β ∈ [[1, µ]]} ∪ {bβ , cβ , β ∈ WS \ {α} \ {α}}
contribute to the statement (5.37). Thus, combining (5.44) with the estimate |B1 ∪ B2 | = Oq (1) from (5.21)
and (5.22), the estimate (5.37) follows.

5.4     Stability under switching
We recall the cells from Definition 5.13, and the set of switching data S(G) from Section 3. In this section,
we derive estimates for the Green’s function G̃(T) of the graph G̃ (T) , which is the switched graph obtained
from G̃ with vertices T removed. Before stating Proposition 5.18 , we need to introduce some sets.

Definition 5.16. We define the set F2 (G) ⊂ S(G) of switching data S, such that for the switching data
S ∈ S(G), the following holds:
  (i) All except for Oq (1) of the vertices {c1 , c2 , . . . , cµ } have radius-R tree neighborhoods in G (T) , i.e. (5.23)
      holds.
 (ii) The vertices {a1 , . . . , aµ , b1 , . . . , bµ , c1 , · · · , cµ } do not cluster in the sense of graph distance, i.e. (5.20)–
      (5.22) hold.
(iii) The vertices {b1 , b2 , . . . , bµ , c1 , c2 , · · · , cµ } do not cluster in the sense of the Green’s function distance,
      i.e. (5.36) and (5.37) hold.
    Then, for any G ∈ Ω+
                       o (z), we have

                                                  PG (F2 (G)) = 1 − O(N −q ).                                                (5.45)

Indeed, (i) and (ii) follow from Proposition 5.10, (iii) follows from Proposition 5.14.

Definition 5.17. We recall the admissible set WS ⊂ [[1, µ]] from (3.2), and define U ⊂ WS to be the set that
for any α ∈ U the following holds:
   • In G (T) , cα has a radius-R tree neighborhood;
   • There exists some 1 ⩽ s ⩽ κ, such that Ss = {bα , cα }. In other words, the cell containing {bα , cα } is
     Ss = {bα , cα }.
    For S ∈ F2 (G), at least µ − Oq (1) edges are switchable:

                                                    ν = |WS | ⩾ µ − Oq (1).                                                  (5.46)


                                                                32
Especially, for α ̸= β ∈ WS , we have
                   distG (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }), distG̃ (T) ({aα , bα , cα }, {aβ , bβ , cβ }) ⩾ R/4.       (5.47)
Moreover, Proposition 5.11 implies that (5.28) and (5.29) hold. Finally thanks to Proposition 5.14, we have
that the size of the set U is at least µ − O(log N ). The results of this section are the following stability
estimates.

Proposition 5.18. Let d ⩾ 3, ωd as in Definition 2.6 and z ∈ C+ with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N . Let G ∈ Ω(z)
or Ω+o (z) as defined in Definitions 4.4 and 4.5 respectively. Then there exists an event F2 (G) ⊂ S(G) as in
Definition 5.16 with PG (F2 (G)) = 1 − O(N −q ), such that for any S ∈ F2 (G) with G̃ = TS (G) the following

Remark 5.19 (Remark on Methods). The Remark 5.8 can be applied to estimate G(TWS ) from G(T) . We
had also used the Ward identity (5.33) and Green’s function correlated notion. Once G(TWS ) is estimated,
we note that by definition G(TWS ) = G̃(TWS ) . Thus our next task is to estimate G̃(T) from G(TWS ) . For
i, j ∈ WS , we use the Schur complement formulas (5.63) and (5.64). For i ∈ WS , j ∈ [[N ]] \ TWS , we have
the Schur complement formula (5.66). Finally, for i, j ∈ [[N ]] \ T, we use the Schur complement formula
(5.68). Similar ideas will be applied to estimate G̃ from bounds on G̃(T) . We have thus bounded G̃ from
bounds on G through the path

                                 G −→ G(T) −→ G(TWS ) = G̃(TWS ) −→ G̃(T) −→ G̃.

Notice that in each step in this path most estimates deteriorate (in the sense that most estimates get worse
by constant factors) and hence this procedure only provides stability bounds on the Green’s functions. In
order to use continuity arguments, we will need to improve the final estimates by a small constant factor
in certain key terms (otherwise after a few iterations the constants will increase exponentially). The key
improvement will be achieved by a concentration argument exploring the fact that the switching data are
essentially independent variables. This will be done in Section 6.

5.5    Stability estimate for the switched graph
In this section we prove the following proposition, which gives the estimates on the Green’s function of the
switched graph G̃.

5.7     Proof of Proposition 4.6
Proof of Proposition 4.6. The event F1 (G) and F2 (G) are constructed in Proposition 5.12 and in Definition
5.16 respectively, and they satisfy P(F1 (G)∩F2 (G)) = 1−O(N −q ). Proposition 5.12 states that for S ∈ F1 (G),
G̃ = TS (G) ∈ Ω̄+ . The definition of spectral regular graphs Ω(z) ⊂ Ω̄ as in Definition 4.4 and Proposition
5.1 implies that Ω(z) ⊂ Ω+ o (z). Proposition 5.22 implies that for S ∈ F2 (G), G̃ = TS (G) satisfies

           |Q(G̃, z) − msc (z)| ⩽ |Q(G, z) − msc (z)| + |Q(G, z) − Q(G̃, z)|

      ≲ |Gij (Ext(Br ({T, i, j}, G̃), Q(G, z)), z) − Gij (Ext(Br ({T, i, j}, G̃), Q(G̃, z)), z)|
      + |Gij (Ext(Br ({T, i, j}, G̃), Q(G̃, z)), z) − Gij (Ext(Br ({i, j}, G̃), Q(G̃, z)), z)| ≲ |Q(G, z) − Q(G̃, z)|

6      Proof of Proposition 4.8: Improved Green’s Function Estimates
In this Section, we prove Proposition 4.8 by a concentration from local resampling: if the Green’s functions
of G or G (T) satisfy certain estimates, the Green’s functions of the switched graphs, i.e. G̃, satisfy improved
estimates. In Section 6.1, i.e. Proposition 6.1, we will show that weighted averages of the Green’s function of
G̃ (T) over the vertex boundary of T concentrate under the local resampling. This concentration will be used
in (6.24) to improve estimate for the difference G̃oi − Poi . This provides the key improvements on estimates
of Green’s functions to implement continuity argument. These improvements were stated as Proposition 4.8
and will be proved in Section 6.2.


6.1      Concentration in the switched graph
We recall the notations and definitions from Section 3 and Section 4. Our local resampling involves a fixed
center vertex o, and a radius ℓ. Given a d-regular graph G, we abbreviate T = Bℓ (o, G) (which may not be a
tree) and its vertex set T. We enumerate edge boundary ∂E T as ∂E T = {e1 , e2 , . . . , eµ }, where eα = {lα , aα }
with lα ∈ T and aα ∈ [[N ]] \ T. We recall that I = {ã1 , ã2 , . . . , ãµ } denotes the set of outer boundary vertices
in the switched graph G̃, where ãα = aα if α ∈ [[1, µ]] \ WS and ãα = cα if α ∈ WS . We also recall the
definition of Q(G, z) from (4.8) and the function Yr (Q(G, z), z) from (2.14).
    The result of this section is the following proposition, which asserts that the average of the Green’s
function of G̃ (T) over the vertex boundary of T concentrates under the local resampling. This is where the
condition that the edge boundary contains more than log N edges is crucial.

      A = {α ∈ [[1, µ]]: path from o to ℓα does not pass through k}.
