7      Eigenvalue Rigidity
We recall the control parameters ε(z), ε′ (z) from Section 4. In this section we prove the following improved
bound for the Stieltjes transform mN (z) of the eigenvalues of G. The bound of mN (z)−md (z) in the following
theorem is the square of the error bound in Theorem 4.2. The improved bound implies that there are no
eigenvalues (except for the trivial one) of G outside the interval [−2 − N −Ω(1) , 2 + N −Ω(1) ].

Theorem 7.1 (Improved Local Law). Fix d ⩾ 3, c > 0, and recall the set of radius-R tree like graphs
Ω̄ ⊂ GN,d from Definition 1.1. For any large C > 0, small e > 0 and N large enough, with probability
1 − O(N −C ) with respect to the uniform measure on Ω̄, the Stieltjes transform of the eigenvalues of G
satisfies: for any z ∈ C+ such that Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b and ε(z) ⩽ (κ(z) + η(z))/ log N ,
                                            1
                 mN (z) − md (z) −       √
                                   N (d/ d − 1 − z)
                                                                                                             (7.1)
                                                                     p            !1/2 
                        e
                ≲p
                      N        (ε(z))2 + (ε(z))1/2 Im[md (z)] + ε(z)/ κ(z) + η(z)      .
                   κ(z) + η(z)                                   N η(z)


   In Section 7.1, we prove a key proposition, which is reminiscent the Fluctuation Averaging Lemma for
Wigner matrices [29, 39]. Using Proposition 7.2 as input, we give the proof of Theorem 7.1 in Section 7.2.
Our main Theorem 1.2 on the rigidity of eigenvalues is a consequence of Theorem 4.2 and 7.1.

7.1     Fluctuation Averaging Proposition
We recall the set Ω(z) of spectral regular graphs from Definition 4.4. A d-regularpgraph G ∈ Ω(z) then G ∈ Ω̄
as in Definition 1.1, and its Green’s function satisfies |Q(G, z) − msc (z)| ⩽ ε(z)/ κ(z) + η(z) + ε(z) and the
relation |Gij (z) − Gij (Ext(Br (i, j, G), Q(G, z)), z)| ⩽ ε(z) for any two vertices i, j ∈ [[N ]].
    For any finite d-regular graph G, we recall the quantity Q from (4.8)
                                                    1 X X (j)
                                       Q(G, z) =              Gii (G, z),                                    (7.2)
                                                   Nd   j:i∼j
                                                       i∈[[N ]]


G(j) (G, z) is the Green’s function of the graph obtained from G by removing the vertex j. The quantity
Q(G, z) satisfies the following self-consistent equation:

                                             Q(G, z) ≈ Yℓ (Q(G, z), z),                                      (7.3)

where the function Yℓ is as defined in (2.14). The equation w = Yℓ (w, z) has a fix point at w = msc (z). In
the proof of Proposition 4.12, by averaging (4.16) over all o ∈ [[N ]], we get with high probability
                                                                 log N ε′ (z)
                                                                             
                                Q(G, z) − Yℓ (Q(G, z), z) = O                   .                      (7.4)
                                                                 (d − 1)ℓ/2
In this section, we obtain the following estimate on the high moments of Q(G, z) −√Yℓ (Q(G, z), z) − δQ (z),
where δQ (z) is a deterministic quantity corresponding to the trivial eigenvalue at d/ d − 1 defined in (7.6).
This new estimate, roughly speaking, improves the error in (7.4) to its square.

Proposition 7.2. For any d ⩾ 3 fixed, the Green’s function of a random d-regular graph satisfies that, for
any z ∈ C+ with Im[z] ⩾ (log N )b /N , and integer p ⩾ 1,

    The proof of Proposition 7.2 relies on the following lemma, which will be used to perform a discrete
integration by part, when computing the expectation on the high moments of Q(G, z) − Yℓ (Q(G, z), z) −
δQ (z).

Lemma 7.3. Fix d ⩾ 3. We recall the operator TS from (3.3). Let G be a random d-regular graph and S
uniformly distributed over S(G), then the graph pair (G, TS (G)) forms an exchangeable pair:

                                                                  law
                                                  (G, TS (G)) = (TS (G), G).

Proof. Lemma The statement that (G, TS (G)) and (TS (G), G) have the same law follows from

                                  P(G = G1 , TS (G) = G2 ) = P(TS (G) = G1 , G = G2 ),                           (7.7)

for any two d-regular graphs G1 , G2 ∈ GN,d . In the following we prove (7.7). If Bℓ (o, G1 ) ̸= Bℓ (o, G2 ), then
both sides of (7.7) are zero. There is nothing to prove. Otherwise, Bℓ (o, G1 ) = Bℓ (o, G2 ), which implies
that |S(G1 )| = |S(G2 )|. We recall T (S) from (3.4). It is proven in Proposition 3.7 that the operator T is an
involution, i.e. TS (G1 ) = G2 if and only if TT (S) (G2 ) = G1 . This implies

                                                                  1       1        X
                            P(G = G1 , TS (G) = G2 ) =                                       1(TS (G1 ) = G2 )
                                                               |GN,d | |S(G1 )|
                                                                                  S∈S(G1 )
                              1        1         X
                        =                                 1(TT (S) (G2 ) = G1 )
                            |GN,d | |S(G2 )|
                                               S∈S(G1 )
                              1        1         X
                        =                                 1(TS (G2 ) = G1 ) = P(G = G2 , TS (G) = G1 ),
                            |GN,d | |S(G1 )|
                                               S∈S(G2 )


which finishes the proof of Lemma 7.3.

Remark 7.4. Thanks to Proposition 3.3, the local resampling of the edge boundaries of the radius-ℓ neigh-
borhoods of o is a reversible Markov chain. Lemma 7.3 is also an immediate consequence of the reversibility.

A      Properties of the Green’s functions
Throughout this paper, we repeatedly use some (well-known) identities for Green’s functions, which we
collect in this appendix.


                                                        60
A.1     Resolvent identity
The following well-known identity is referred as resolvent identity: for two invertible matrices A and B of
the same size, we have
                           A−1 − B −1 = A−1 (B − A)B −1 = B −1 (B − A)A−1 .                           (A.1)

A.2     Schur complement formula
Given an N × N matrix M and an index set T ⊂ [[N ]], recall that we denote by M |T the T × T-matrix
obtained by restricting M to T, and that by M (T) = M |Tc , where Tc = [[N ]] \ T, we denote the matrix
obtained by removing the rows and columns corresponding to indices in T. Thus, for any T ⊂ [[N ]], any
symmetric matrix H can be written (up to rearrangement of indices) in the block form
                                                  A B′
                                                      
                                           H=            ,
                                                 B D

with A = H|T and D = H (T) . The Schur complement formula asserts that, for any z ∈ C+ ,
                             (A − B ′ G(T) B)−1         −(A − B ′ G(T) B)−1 B ′ G(T)
                                                                                          
       G = (H − z)−1 =                                                                       ,         (A.2)
                        −G(T) B(A − B ′ G(T) B)−1 G(T) + G(T) B(A − B ′ G(T) B)−1 B ′ G(T)

where G(T) = (D − z)−1 . Throughout the paper, we often use the following special cases of (A.2):
                                               G|T = (A − B ′ G(T) B)−1 ,
                                     G|Tc − G(T) = G|Tc T (G|T )−1 G|TTc ,                             (A.3)
                                                                 ′   (T)
                                           G|TTc = −G|T B G                ,
as well as the special case
                                               (k)            Gik Gkj
                                           Gij = Gij −                .                                (A.4)
                                                               Gkk

A.3     Ward identity
For any symmetric N ×N matrix H, its Green’s function G(z) = (H −z)−1 satisfies the Ward identity
                                         N
                                         X                    Im Gjj (z)
                                               |Gij (z)|2 =              ,                             (A.5)
                                         j=1
                                                                  η
                                                             PN
where η = Im[z]. This identity provides a bound for the sum j=1 |Gij (z)|2 in terms of the diagonal entries
of the Green’s function. For an explanation why this algebraic identity has the interpretation of a Ward, see
e.g. [74, p.147].

A.4     Covering map
Given a graph G (which might be infinite), with vertex set G and Green’s function G. For any vertex i, the
vector (Gi1 , Gi2 , Gi3 , . . . ) ∈ ℓ2 (G) is uniquely determined by the following relations:
                                                               1     X
                                                 1 + zGii = √             Gik
                                                              d − 1 k:i∼k
                                                                                                    (A.6)
                                                               1     X
                                                    zGij = √              Gik
                                                             d − 1 k:j∼k

where i ∼ k denotes that i and k are adjacent in G, i.e., that Aik = Aki = 1.

Lemma A.1. Given a covering π : G̃ → G of graphs, denote the Green’s function of G̃ by G̃ and that of G
by G. Then for all vertices i, j in G, the Green’s functions obey
                                                      X
                                              Gij =         G̃xy ,                                (A.7)
                                                      y:π(y)=j

where x is any vertex of G̃ with π(x) = i, provided the righthand side of (A.7) converges.


                                                       61
Proof. See [10, Lemma B.1].


Proposition A.2. For any d ⩾ 3, ωd defined in 2.9 satisfies ωd ⩾ 1.


Proof.PBy Definition 2.6, ωd ⩾ 1 if for any connected graph G with vertex set G and deficit function g such
that v∈G g(v) + excess(G) ⩽ 1, (2.9) and (2.10) hold. For any z ∈ C+ , from (2.4), we have

                           |msc (z)| ≍ |md (z)| ≍ 1,        Im[msc (z)] ≍ Im[md (z)] ⩾ 0.
   P
If Pv∈G g(v) + excess(G) = 0, then TE(G) is the infinite d-regular tree, (2.9) and (2.10) follow from (2.5).
If v∈G g(v) =  P1 and excess(G) = 0, then TE(G) is the infinite (d − 1)-ary tree, (2.9) and (2.10) follow
from (2.6). If v∈G g(v) = 0 and excess(G) = 1, then TE(G) is an infinite d-regular graph with exact one
cycle. The infinite d-regular tree X is the universal covering of G, π : X 7→ G. The Green’s function of X is
computed in (2.5). Lemma A.1 implies for any vertices i, j ∈ G, let x be any vertex of X with π(x) = i, we
have
                                                                          distX (x,y)
                                                   X             msc (z)
                                 Gij = md (z)                   −√                        .                       (A.8)
                                                 y:π(y)=j
                                                                   d−1

Each vertex y with π(y) = j corresponds to a non-backtracking path from i to j, and distX (x, y) is the
length of the non-backtracking path. Since G contains exact one cycle, we denote it as C. The lengths
of non-backtracking paths from i to j form arithmetic progressions with a common difference of |C| ⩾ 3.
Therefore, the righthand side (A.8) converges, (2.9) and (2.10) follow.
